The Landau–Lifshitz–Bloch equation on polytopal domains: Unique existence and finite element approximation
Kim-Ngan Le, Agus L. Soenjaya, Thanh Tran

Abstract
. The Landau–Lifshitz–Bloch equation (LLBE) describes the evolution of the magnetic spin field in ferromagnets at high temperatures. In this paper, we study the numerical approximation of the LLBE in the regime above the Curie temperature on bounded polytopal domains in ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} , with d ∈ { 1 , 2 , 3 } d\in\{1,2,3\} , allowing for non-convex domains when d = 2 d=2 . We first establish the existence and uniqueness of strong solutions to the LLBE and propose a linear, fully discrete, conforming finite element scheme for its approximation. While this scheme is shown to converge, the obtained rate is suboptimal. To address this shortcoming, we introduce a viscous (pseudo-parabolic) regularisation of the LLBE, which we call the ϵ \epsilon -LLBE. For this regularised problem, we prove the unique existence of strong solutions and establish a rate of convergence of the solution 𝒖 ϵ \boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon} of the ϵ \epsilon -LLBE to the solution 𝒖 \boldsymbol{u} of the LLBE as ϵ → 0 + \epsilon\to 0^{+} . Furthermore, we propose a linear, fully discrete, conforming finite element scheme to approximate the solution of the ϵ \epsilon -LLBE. Given sufficiently smooth initial data, error analysis is performed to show stability and uniform-in-time convergence of the scheme. Finally, several numerical simulations are presented to corroborate our theoretical results.



1.
Introduction

Micromagnetic modeling has proved itself to be an effective theoretical framework in describing magnetisation dynamics at sub-micrometre length scales.
The framework of Landau–Lifshitz–Bloch equation
(LLBE)
(
Garanin, 1991
;
Garanin, 1997
)
overcomes
a problem of the standard model of magnetisation which carries a significant
limitation of being valid only at very low temperatures,
far below the Curie temperature
T_{\text{c}}

(
Chubykalo-Fesenko and Nieves, 2020
)
.
The LLBE essentially interpolates between the standard model at low temperatures and
the Ginzburg-Landau theory of phase transitions.
It is valid not only below, but also above the temperature
T_{\text{c}}
. The LLBE has been successfully used to analyse magnetisation dynamics at high temperature
(
Atxitia et al., 2016
;
Chubykalo-Fesenko and Nieves, 2020
;
Chubykalo-Fesenko et al., 2006
;
Nieves and Chubykalo-Fesenko, 2016
)
. It is also widely used in the physics literature to model the heat-assisted magnetic recording (HAMR), a modern hard drive technology recently made available commercially
(
Meo et al., 2020
;
Zhu and Li, 2013
)
.

In this paper, we consider a deterministic form of the LLBE
in which the temperature is raised higher than
T_{\text{c}}
. We assume that the effective field is dominated by exchange interactions, although it is straightforward to include the lower-order uniaxial anisotropy field and a time-varying external field in the equation.
The macroscopic magnetisation
\boldsymbol{u}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
at time
t\geq 0
and

\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathscr{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
d=1,2,3
, satisfies the
problem
(
Chubykalo-Fesenko et al., 2006
;
Le, 2016
)
:

(1.1a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}=\kappa_{1}\Delta\boldsymbol{u}+\gamma\boldsymbol{u}\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}-\kappa_{2}(1+\mu|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2})\boldsymbol{u}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }(0,T)\times\mathscr{D},

(1.1b)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}(0,\cdot)=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\mathscr{D},

(1.1c)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{u}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\nu}}=0\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial\mathscr{D}.

Here,
\partial\mathscr{D}
denotes the boundary of a bounded polytopal domain
\mathscr{D}
, which is assumed to be convex when
d=3
, but can be non-convex when
d=2
. Moreover,
\boldsymbol{\nu}
is the outward normal unit vector.
The positive coefficients
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
are phenomenological temperature-dependent damping
parameters, while
\gamma
is the gyromagnetic ratio (which can be positive or
negative depending on the precessional direction of the particle). The positive constant

\mu
is proportional to
T_{\mathrm{c}}/(\Theta-T_{\mathrm{c}})
, where
\Theta
is the ferromagnet temperature. Although the LLBE can be coupled to a thermal transport equation to incorporate temperature dynamics
(
Atxitia et al., 2010
)
, we consider here an isothermal regime and treat the temperature as a fixed parameter.
At this juncture, we refer the reader to Section
2.1
for further notations that are used in this paper.

Some available mathematical results related to the theoretical or numerical analysis for the LLBE will be reviewed next. The existence of a weak solution to the LLBE in smooth bounded domains is shown in
(
Le, 2016
)
, while the unique existence of a strong solution to the LLBE coupled with the Maxwell equation is studied in
(
Li et al., 2021
)
for 2-D periodic domains. To the
best of our knowledge, existence and uniqueness of
strong
solutions to the LLBE on
polytopal
domains have not been studied before.

In the context of numerical analysis for the LLBE, several finite element schemes have recently been proposed to solve (
1.1
). A nonlinear finite element scheme is introduced in
(
Benmouane et al., 2024
)
, where conditional weak convergence is established, albeit without a convergence rate and under much stronger assumptions on the mesh than those considered in this work. Another scheme is presented in
(
Soenjaya, 2025
)
, which employs a different regularisation strategy (specifically, a fourth-order Baryakhtar-type
(
Soenjaya and Tran, 2023
)
regularisation) to develop an energy-stable mixed finite element method. This method achieves unconditional convergence with optimal rates. However, both schemes are nonlinear in the sense that a system of nonlinear equations must be solved at each time step. Moreover, the method in
(
Soenjaya, 2025
)
requires very strong regularity assumptions on the exact solution.

Our first contribution in this paper is to address these gaps by first establishing the well-posedness of the LLBE on polytopal domains, extending the results of
(
Le, 2016
)
in several directions: we allow non-smooth domains, prove the unique existence of strong (and more regular) solutions, and derive a decay estimate as the time variable
t\to\infty
. Building on this analytical foundation, we propose a linear finite element scheme for approximating the solution to (
1.1
) directly. In contrast to the aforementioned schemes in
(
Benmouane et al., 2024
;
Soenjaya, 2025
)
, our method is linear and requires solving only a linear system at each time step, resulting in significantly reduced computational cost. Moreover, under an adequate regularity assumption on the initial data, we prove the convergence of the scheme. We note that unlike the standard Landau–Lifshitz equation, whose solutions are constrained to lie on the unit sphere
(
Gilbert, 1955
;
Landau and Lifshitz, 1935
)
, a distinguishing feature of the LLBE is that the magnetisation magnitude is not conserved.
Consequently, numerical methods developed for the standard Landau–Lifshitz equation, which crucially rely on the sphere constraint, are not applicable to the LLBE.

The linear finite element scheme mentioned above (see Section
4
) requires a restriction on the ratio of time-step size to mesh size, assumes strong regularity of the exact solution, and yields only suboptimal convergence rates due to a lack of stability of the discrete solution in
\mathbb{L}^{\infty}
and higher-order Sobolev norms. We note that a more stringent condition is imposed in
(
Benmouane et al., 2024
)
.
To address these shortcomings, we introduce a viscous regularisation of (
1.1
) by adding the term
-\epsilon\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}
, where
\epsilon\in(0,1)
is a small
parameter. The resulting equation, satisfied by the vector field

\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}:(0,T)\times\mathscr{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
, is called the
\epsilon
-LLBE. This regularised problem is formulated as:

(1.2a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}-\epsilon\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}=\kappa_{1}\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}+\gamma\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}-\kappa_{2}(1+\mu|\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}|^{2})\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }(0,T)\times\mathscr{D},

(1.2b)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(0,\cdot)=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\epsilon}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\mathscr{D},

(1.2c)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\nu}}=0\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial\mathscr{D}.

Our second contribution in this paper is to show a rate of convergence of the solution

\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}
of (
1.2
) to the
solution
\boldsymbol{u}
of (
1.1
) as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, provided the initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\epsilon}
is appropriately chosen (for instance
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\epsilon}=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
). Similar ideas to
approximate the solution of a PDE by its viscous (pseudo-parabolic)
regularisation have been applied before, for instance in
(
Novick-Cohen and Pego, 1991
;
Showalter and Ting, 1970
)
for a
class of nonlinear parabolic equations, as well as in
(
Benjamin et al., 1972
)
and
(
Ang and Tran, 1990
)
for the BBM
equation.

Our third contribution in this paper is the development of a stable and optimally convergent linear finite element scheme for the regularised
\epsilon
-LLBE. In contrast to previous works, it is worth emphasising that we consider exclusively polytopal domains, allowing for re-entrant corners in the two-dimensional case.

To support the numerical analysis, we prove the unique existence of global strong solutions to the
\epsilon
-LLBE by deriving key energy estimates, which are obtained by combining elliptic regularity theorems and embedding properties of fractional Sobolev spaces in Lipschitz domains. It is known that in domains with re-entrant corners, the singularity at the corner pollutes the finite element solution everywhere if globally quasi-uniform triangulations are used, leading to a lower order of convergence
(
Chatzipantelidis et al., 2006
;
Li and Zhang, 2017
)
. We confirm this behaviour for the problem at hand, and further derive an error estimate that holds uniformly in time, analogous to the result developed in
(
Larsson, 1989
)
for semilinear parabolic equations. Notably, the extension to our highly nonlinear setting is nontrivial.

We remark that another regularisation (by adding the term
\epsilon\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}
) is considered in
(
Goldys et al., 2025
)
for the stochastic LLBE, turning the equation into a
fourth-order nonlinear stochastic PDE. A numerical method is then proposed to
solve the equation and the rate of convergence to the exact solution is shown
for
d\in\{1,2\}
. Since we intend to work with a
conforming finite element method, such regularisation is not utilised here to avoid using a
C^{1}
-conforming finite element for a
fourth-order problem, which can be computationally costly and difficult to
implement, especially for
d=3
.

In summary, the main contributions of this paper are the following results:

(1)

existence and uniqueness of strong and regular solutions
\boldsymbol{u}
to (
1.1
) in polytopal domains (Theorem
3.6
and
3.7
);

(2)

long-term behaviour of
\boldsymbol{u}
,
more precisely,
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\mathscr{D})}\leq e^{-\kappa_{2}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\mathscr{D})}
(Theorem
3.8
);

(3)

suboptimal error estimates of the finite element approximation (Algorithm
4.1
) to the solution of (
1.1
) with a realistic assumption on the regularity of the solution (Theorem
4.4
);

(4)

existence and uniqueness of strong and regular solutions
\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}

to the regularised problem (
1.2
) in polytopal domains (Theorem
5.5
and
5.6
);

(5)

rate of convergence in
L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathscr{D}))
of

\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}
to the solution
\boldsymbol{u}
of (
1.1
) as

\epsilon\to 0^{+}
(Theorem
5.7
);

(6)

optimal error estimates of the finite element approximation (Algorithm
6.1
) to the solution of the
\epsilon
-LLBE problem (
1.2
) with a realistic assumption on the solution regularity (Theorem
6.3
and
6.4
);

(7)

uniform-in-time error estimates of
the proposed linear finite element scheme for
the
\epsilon
-LLBE problem (Theorem
7.3
),
which are verified by a series of numerical experiments in Section
8
.

It is worth noting that physical theory indicates a phase transition from the ferromagnetic to the paramagnetic state above the Curie temperature
T_{\mathrm{c}}
, where the equilibrium net magnetisation vanishes
(
Chubykalo-Fesenko et al., 2006
;
Kazantseva et al., 2008
;
Vogler et al., 2014
)
. The long-time behaviour of the macroscopic magnetisation
\boldsymbol{u}
, as established in Theorem
3.8
, is consistent with this result.

We remark that if
\mathscr{D}
has a smooth boundary, then we may approximate the boundary
\partial\mathscr{D}
with a polytopal boundary
\partial\mathscr{D}_{h}
. Since
\partial\mathscr{D}_{h}
may not coincide with
\partial\mathscr{D}
, additional domain approximation assumptions and more careful analysis are required to deal with the resulting discrepancy; see, e.g.
(
Thomée, 1973
;
Lin et al., 1991
)
. As discussed in
(
Schatz et al., 1998
, Section 2)
, one approach is to take the mesh domain slightly larger than
\mathscr{D}
and restrict the computed solution back to
\mathscr{D}
. For natural boundary conditions, the associated error is comparable to that of a suitable quadrature rule. A detailed treatment of this issue is beyond the scope of this paper.

The paper is organised as follows. In the next section, we introduce the notation, assumptions, and auxiliary results used throughout. Section
3
establishes the existence of a weak solution and a unique strong solution to the LLBE. In Section
4
, we propose a linear finite element discretisation of the LLBE and prove a suboptimal convergence rate. To improve this, we consider the
\epsilon
-LLBE in Section
5
. Assuming attainable regularity of the exact solution, we develop a finite element method for the regularised problem in Section
6
and establish a uniform-in-time error estimate in Section
7
. Section
8
presents numerical simulations that support the theoretical results. Additional auxiliary results and technical estimates are provided in Appendices
A
and
B
.

2.
Preliminaries

We first introduce some notations in the following section. Further assumptions and auxiliary results used in this paper are collected in Section
2.2
and Appendix
A
, respectively.

2.1.
Notations

Notations used throughout this paper are clarified in this section. For brevity, we denote by
\partial_{j}
the spatial partial derivative
\partial/\partial x_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,d
, and by
\partial_{t}
the temporal partial derivative
\partial/\partial t
. We also denote by
\Delta
the Neumann Laplacian operator on a bounded domain
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
d=1,2,3
.

For
p\in[1,\infty]
and
k=1,2,\ldots
, the spaces
\mathbb{L}^{p}(\mathscr{D})
and
\mathbb{W}^{k,p}(\mathscr{D})
denote the usual Lebesgue and Sobolev spaces of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
-valued functions on
\mathscr{D}
. In particular, we use the convention
\mathbb{W}^{0,p}(\mathscr{D})\equiv\mathbb{L}^{p}(\mathscr{D})
and
\mathbb{H}^{k}(\mathscr{D}):=\mathbb{W}^{k,2}(\mathscr{D})
. The norm in a Banach space
X
is denoted by
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{X}
. The inner product of
\mathbb{L}^{2}
vector-valued functions and the inner product of
\mathbb{L}^{2}
matrix-valued functions will not be distinguished, and are denoted by
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
. Also, for
k\in\{1,2\}
, we define the space

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{k}_{\Delta}(\mathscr{D}):=\big\{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{k}(\mathscr{D}):\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{k-1}(\mathscr{D})\big\},

which is a Banach space for the obvious norm

(2.2)

\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{k}_{\Delta}(\mathscr{D})}:=\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{k}(\mathscr{D})}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{k-1}(\mathscr{D})}.

Note that in general
\mathbb{H}^{k+1}(\mathscr{D})\subset\mathbb{H}^{k}_{\Delta}(\mathscr{D})
, but
\mathbb{H}^{k}_{\Delta}(\mathscr{D})\not\subset\mathbb{H}^{k+1}(\mathscr{D})
.

We shall need to use fractional Sobolev spaces in the analysis. For any
s=m+\sigma
, where
m
is a non-negative integer and
\sigma\in(0,1)
, we define the Sobolev–Slobodeckij norm

\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}(\mathscr{D})}:=\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{m}}^{2}+\sum_{|\alpha|=m}\iint_{\mathscr{D}\times\mathscr{D}}\frac{|\partial^{\alpha}\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{x})-\partial^{\alpha}\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{y})|^{2}}{|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|^{d+2\sigma}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{y}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

and let
\mathbb{H}^{s}(\mathscr{D})
be the completion of
C^{\infty}(\overline{\mathscr{D}})
with respect to this norm, see for instance
(
Di Nezza et al., 2012
;
Grisvard, 2011
;
Stephan and Tran, 2021
)
for a more detailed discussion. We denote by
\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}^{-s}(\mathscr{D})
the dual space of
\mathbb{H}^{s}(\mathscr{D})
with respect to the dual pairing which is the extension of the
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathscr{D})
inner product.

If
\mathbb{X}
is a Banach space, the spaces
L^{p}(0,T;\mathbb{X})
and
W^{k,p}(0,T;\mathbb{X})
denote
respectively the Lebesgue and Sobolev spaces of functions on
(0,T)
taking
values in
\mathbb{X}
. The space
C([0,T];\mathbb{X})
denotes the space of continuous functions
on
[0,T]
taking values in
\mathbb{X}
. For
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
and
k\geq 0
,
we write
\mathbb{L}^{p}
,
\mathbb{H}^{k}
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}^{-k}
,
L^{q}_{T}(\mathbb{L}^{p})
,
L^{q}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{k})
, and
W^{k,q}_{T}(\mathbb{L}^{p})
for
\mathbb{L}^{p}(\mathscr{D})
,
\mathbb{H}^{k}(\mathscr{D})
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}^{-k}(\mathscr{D})
,

L^{q}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{p}(\mathscr{D}))
,
L^{q}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{k}(\mathscr{D}))
, and
W^{k,q}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{p}(\mathscr{D}))
, respectively.

Since the functions involved are vector-valued functions, we need to clarify
the meaning of the dot and cross products of different mathematical objects. For any vector-valued function
\boldsymbol{u}=(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}):\mathscr{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we define

\nabla\boldsymbol{u}:=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1}\boldsymbol{u}&\cdots&\partial_{d}\boldsymbol{u}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1}u_{1}&\cdots&\partial_{d}u_{1}\\
\partial_{1}u_{2}&\cdots&\partial_{d}u_{2}\\
\partial_{1}u_{3}&\cdots&\partial_{d}u_{3}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta\boldsymbol{u}:=\begin{pmatrix}\Delta u_{1}\\
\Delta u_{2}\\
\Delta u_{3}\end{pmatrix}.

For any vector
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and matrices
\boldsymbol{A},\ \boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times d}
, we
define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}

\displaystyle:=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}^{(1)}&\cdots&\boldsymbol{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}^{(d)}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d},\quad

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}\cdot\boldsymbol{B}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\boldsymbol{A}^{(j)}\cdot\boldsymbol{B}^{(j)}\in\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}\times\boldsymbol{A}

\displaystyle:=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{z}\times\boldsymbol{A}^{(1)}&\cdots&\boldsymbol{z}\times\boldsymbol{A}^{(d)}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times d},\qquad

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}\times\boldsymbol{B}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\boldsymbol{A}^{(j)}\times\boldsymbol{B}^{(j)}\in\mathbb{R}^{3},

where
\boldsymbol{A}^{(j)}
and
\boldsymbol{B}^{(j)}
denote the
j^{\rm th}
- column
of
\boldsymbol{A}
and
\boldsymbol{B}
, respectively.
Hence, if
\boldsymbol{u}
,
\boldsymbol{v}
, and
\boldsymbol{w}
are
vector-valued functions defined on
\mathscr{D}
and taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, then

(2.3)

\displaystyle 2\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}=\nabla\left(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1}(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2})&\cdots&\partial_{d}(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2})\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{w}=\big(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{v}\big)\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{w}+\big(\boldsymbol{u}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\big)\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{w},

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{u}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{w}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\big(\boldsymbol{u}\times\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{v}\big)\cdot\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{w},

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\partial_{j}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\partial_{j}\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla v_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x},

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}=\int_{\mathscr{D}}(\boldsymbol{u}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathscr{D}}\big(\boldsymbol{u}\times\partial_{j}\boldsymbol{v}\big)\cdot\partial_{j}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

Finally, throughout this paper, the constant
c
in an estimate denotes a
generic constant which may take different values at different occurrences. If
the dependence of
c
on some variable, e.g.
T
, needs to be highlighted, we will write

c_{T}
or
c(T)
. The notation
A\lesssim B
means
A\leq cB
, where the specific form of
the constant
c
is not important to clarify.

2.2.
Assumptions

Throughout this paper, we assume that the bounded polytopal domain
\mathscr{D}
is either:

(1)

a two-dimensional polygonal domain (which may be
non-convex
), or

(2)

a three-dimensional
convex
polyhedral domain.

In case
\mathscr{D}
is non-convex, for simplicity of presentation, we assume that
\mathscr{D}
has exactly one re-entrant corner with angle
\omega\in(\pi,2\pi)
, and define the elliptic regularity index

(2.4)

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}:=\pi/\omega.

Observe that
\alpha_{0}\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
. In particular, since
\alpha_{0}>\frac{1}{2}
, we exclude domains with cracks from our discussion. Let
\alpha
be an arbitrary constant satisfying

(2.5)

\displaystyle 1/2<\alpha<\alpha_{0},

\displaystyle\text{if $\mathscr{D}$ is a two-dimensional non-convex polygonal domain},

\displaystyle\alpha=1,

\displaystyle\text{if $\mathscr{D}$ is a convex domain}.

2.3.
Definitions of solutions

The notion of solutions to the
\epsilon
-LLBE and the LLBE needs to be clarified. To motivate these definitions, first multiplying (
1.2a
) by
\boldsymbol{v}
, then using integration by parts (specifically (
A.1
) and (
A.2
)), we obtain formally,

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle+\epsilon\left\langle\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t),\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\kappa_{1}\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t),\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t),\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\kappa_{2}\left\langle(1+\mu|\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)|^{2})\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}.

Note that the terms on the right-hand side are well-defined
if
\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}\in H^{1}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1})\cap L^{2}_{T}(\mathbb{W}^{1,4})

and
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}
. We also note that the embedding
\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{W}^{1,4}
holds for
\alpha
satisfying (
2.5
). Hence, we define the solution of (
1.1
)
and of (
1.2
) as follows.

Definition 2.1

(solution of the
\epsilon
-LLBE)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}_{\Delta}
, and
\alpha_{0}
be as defined in (
2.4
).

(1)

For any
\alpha
satisfying (
2.5
), a function
\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}\in H^{1}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1})\cap L^{2}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha})

is a
weak solution
of the
\epsilon
-LLBE (
1.2
) if, for any
t\in(0,T)
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\epsilon\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t),\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\kappa_{1}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(s),\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\gamma\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(s)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(s),\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s+\kappa_{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle(1+\mu|\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(s)|^{2})\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(s),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s

(2.6)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\epsilon\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{0},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathscr{D}).

(2)

A weak solution
\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}
is said to be a
strong solution

if
\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}\in W^{1,\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1})\cap H^{1}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1}_{\Delta})
. In this case, (
1.2
) is satisfied
for all
t\in(0,T)
and almost all
x\in\mathscr{D}
, and we can write

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)-\epsilon\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{0}-\epsilon\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{0}+\kappa_{1}\int_{0}^{t}\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+\gamma\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(s)\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

(2.7)

\displaystyle\quad-\kappa_{2}\int_{0}^{t}(1+\mu|\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(s)|^{2})\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\quad\text{ in }\,\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathscr{D}).

A weak (resp. strong) solution is said to be a
global
weak (resp. strong) solution if it exists for any given positive time
T
. It is said to be a
local
weak (resp. strong) solution if it only exists up to some particular time
T
.

Definition 2.2

(solution of the LLBE)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}
.

(1)

For any
\alpha
satisfying (
2.5
), a function
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{W}^{1,4/3}_{T}(\mathbb{L}^{2})\cap L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1})\cap L^{2}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha})
is a
weak solution
of the LLBE (
1.1
) if
\boldsymbol{u}(0)=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
and, for any
t\in(0,T)
, (
2.6
) holds with
\epsilon=0
.

(2)

A weak solution
\boldsymbol{u}
is said to be a
strong solution
if
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{W}^{1,\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{L}^{2})\cap L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1}_{\Delta})
. In this case, (
1.1
) is satisfied
for all
t\in(0,T)
and almost all
x\in\mathscr{D}
, and
\boldsymbol{u}
satisfies (
2.7
) with
\epsilon=0
.

It follows from embedding theorems, see e.g.
(
Lions, 1969
, Lemma 1.2)
, that a
weak solution
\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}
of the
\epsilon
-LLBE belongs to
C([0,T];\mathbb{H}^{1})
, while a weak solution
\boldsymbol{u}
of the LLBE only belongs to
C([0,T];\mathbb{L}^{2})
.

3.
Existence and uniqueness of the solution to the LLBE

Throughout this section, let
T>0
be a given number which can be arbitrarily large.
The Faedo–Galerkin method will be used to show the existence of a solution to the LLBE.
This solution satisfies the following weak form: For every
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(t),\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\kappa_{2}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

(3.1a)

\displaystyle=\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}(t)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(t),\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\kappa_{2}\mu\left\langle|\boldsymbol{u}(t)|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{1},

(3.1b)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}(0)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}

Let
\{(\boldsymbol{e}_{i},\lambda_{i})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of eigenpairs of the negative Neumann Laplace operator, i.e., for all
i=1,2,\ldots
,

-\Delta\boldsymbol{e}_{i}=\lambda_{i}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\text{ in }\mathscr{D}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{e}_{i}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\nu}}=0\text{ on }\partial\mathscr{D}.

We can choose the eigenfunctions
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
such that
\{\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
forms an orthonormal
basis of
\mathbb{L}^{2}
.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{n}:=\text{span}\{\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{e}_{n}\}
and let

\Pi_{n}
be the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
-projection onto
\mathcal{S}_{n}
. We consider the following
approximation to (
1.1
): Find
\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(\cdot,t)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}

satisfying

(3.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{1}\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}+\gamma\Pi_{n}\bigl(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\bigr)-\kappa_{2}\Pi_{n}\bigl((1+\mu|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}|^{2})\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle\text{in }(0,T)\times\mathscr{D},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(0)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{u}_{0,n}

\displaystyle\text{in }\mathscr{D}.

We assume that
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
in (
1.1b
) and
\boldsymbol{u}_{0,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
in (
3.2
) are chosen such that the initial data approximation satisfies

(3.3)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0,n}-\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}=0.

Equivalently for every
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t),\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\kappa_{2}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

(3.4a)

\displaystyle=\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\kappa_{2}\mu\left\langle|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{S}_{n},

(3.4b)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(0)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{u}_{0,n}.

The following lemma gives local existence of a solution to (
3.2
).

Lemma 3.1
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a sufficiently small time
\tau_{n}>0
such that the problem (
3.2
)
admits a solution on
[0,\tau_{n}]
.

Proof.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
F_{n}^{i}:\mathcal{S}_{n}\to\mathcal{S}_{n}
,
i=1,2,3
, be defined by

\displaystyle F_{n}^{1}(\boldsymbol{v})=\Delta\boldsymbol{v},\quad F_{n}^{2}(\boldsymbol{v})=\Pi_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}\times\Delta\boldsymbol{v}),\quad F_{n}^{3}(\boldsymbol{v})=\Pi_{n}((1+\mu|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2})\boldsymbol{v}),

for any
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
. It is shown in
(
Le, 2016
, Lemma 3.1)
that
F_{n}^{1}
is
globally Lipschitz, while
F_{n}^{2}
and
F_{n}^{3}
are locally Lipschitz. The local
existence of
\boldsymbol{u}_{n}
follows at once.
∎

We now show some bounds on the local solution
\boldsymbol{u}_{n}

of (
3.2
), which will imply its global existence, i.e., existence of solution on
[0,T]
for any given
T>0
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Assume that (
3.3
) holds. For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha
satisfying (
2.5
),

(3.5)

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq c,

where the constant
c
depends on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}
, but is independent of
n
and
t
.

Proof.

The inequality for the first three terms on the left-hand side of (
3.5
) is shown in
(
Le, 2016
, Lemma 3.2)
. For the last term, by the elliptic regularity estimate (
A.4
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}^{-1+\alpha}}^{2}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}^{-1+\alpha}}^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}s\lesssim 1,

where in the last step we also used the embedding
\mathbb{L}^{2}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}^{-1+\alpha}
and inequality (
3.5
) for the first two terms. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

Assume that (
3.3
) holds.
Then for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the approximate solution
\boldsymbol{u}_{n}
satisfies

(3.6)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4/3}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim 1,

where the constant is independent of
n
.

Proof.

We first derive an estimate for the first term on the left-hand side of (
3.6
). If
d=1
, the inequality for the first term on the left-hand side of (
3.6
) follows by the embedding
\mathbb{H}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{\infty}
and (
3.5
).
For the case
d=2
, by successively applying the Sobolev embedding
\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha/4}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{\infty}
, interpolation inequalities for fractional Sobolev spaces
(
Brezis and Mironescu, 2018
)
, and equation (
3.5
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha/4}}^{4}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha/2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha/2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim 1,

where in the last step we also used the embedding
\mathbb{L}^{2}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}^{-1+\alpha/2}
.
For the case
d=3
, noting that the domain is convex by assumption (and so (
A.5
) holds), by using Agmon’s inequality and (
3.5
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim 1.

Next, taking the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
norm of (
3.2
), then applying Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4/3}

\displaystyle\leq\kappa_{1}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4/3}+\gamma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4/3}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4/3}+\kappa_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4/3}+\kappa_{2}\mu\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}

where in the penultimate step we used the embedding
\mathbb{H}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{6}
, and in the last step we used (
3.5
). Therefore, integrating over
(0,t)
and using (
3.5
) again,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4/3}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}\mathrm{d}s.

By the result established previously, the lemma is proven.
∎

The following lemmas show a uniform bound which hold possibly only locally in time.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
T
be a given positive real number. Assume that

(3.7)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0,n}-\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}_{\Delta}}=0.

holds for
s=1
. Then there exists
T^{\ast}
satisfying

(3.8)

\begin{cases}T^{\ast}=T,\quad&\text{if }d=1,2,\\
T^{\ast}\leq T,\quad&\text{if }d=3,\end{cases}

such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]
,

(3.9)

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq c,

where
c
is independent of
n
.

Proof.

To prove (
3.9
), we
set
\boldsymbol{v}=2\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}

in (
3.4a
) and obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+2\kappa_{1}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+2\kappa_{2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=-2\gamma\left\langle\nabla\Big(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\Big),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+2\kappa_{2}\mu\left\langle\nabla\Big(|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\Big),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=2\gamma\left\langle\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+2\kappa_{2}\mu\left\langle\nabla\Big(|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\Big),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\gamma\left|\left\langle\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\right|+2\kappa_{2}\mu\left|\left\langle\nabla\Big(|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\Big),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\right|

(3.10)

\displaystyle=R_{1}+R_{2},

where

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle:=2\gamma\left|\left\langle\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle:=2\kappa_{2}\mu\left|\left\langle\nabla\Big(|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\Big),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\right|.

Invoking Lemma
A.3
with
\boldsymbol{v}=\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}
,

\boldsymbol{w}=\boldsymbol{u}_{n}
, and
\delta=\kappa_{1}/3d
, we deduce

R_{1}\leq\frac{\kappa_{1}}{3}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\Phi(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t))\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where
\Phi
is defined in (
A.6
).
On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left|\left\langle 2\Big(\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\Big)\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t),\partial_{i}\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\right|+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left|\left\langle|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)|^{2}\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t),\partial_{i}\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\|{\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\partial_{i}\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\kappa_{1}}{3}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+c\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4},

where in the last step we used Young’s inequality and (
3.5
).
Altogether, from (
3.10
) we deduce

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}+\Phi(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t))\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Integrating with respect to
the time variable we deduce, by using (
3.7
) with
s=1
and (
3.6
),

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+c\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}\mathrm{d}s+c\int_{0}^{t}\Phi(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s))\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

(3.11)

\displaystyle\leq c+c\int_{0}^{t}\Phi(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s))\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

It remains to estimate the last term on the right-hand side of (
3.11
). If
d=1
, it is clear from Lemma
A.3
and (
3.5
) that
\Phi(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t))\lesssim 1
.
For the case
d=2
, note that by the Sobolev embedding
\mathbb{H}^{3/2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{W}^{1,4}
, inequality (
A.4
) for
s=1/2
, and the Sobolev interpolation inequality, we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,4}}^{4}\lesssim\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3/2}}^{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}^{-1/2}}^{4}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}^{-1/2}}^{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}^{-1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\left(\sup_{\boldsymbol{0}\neq\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}}\frac{\left|{\left\langle\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t),\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}}\right|}{\left\|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the penultimate step we also used integration by parts (noting the homogeneous Neumann boundary condition for
\boldsymbol{u}_{n}
), and in the last step we again used (
3.5
).
Thus, we infer the following estimates for

d=1,2
and for all
t\in[0,T]
:

(3.12)

\Phi(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t))\lesssim\begin{cases}1,\quad&d=1,\\
1+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},\quad&d=2.\end{cases}

By using
(
3.5
), we infer

(3.13)

\int_{0}^{t}\Phi(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s\lesssim 1,

where the constant is independent of
n
.
By using Gronwall’s inequality and (
3.13
), we deduce (
3.9
) for
d=1,2
.

Finally, consider the case
d=3
. By the Sobolev embedding
\mathbb{H}^{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{W}^{1,6}
and the
\mathbb{H}^{2}
elliptic regularity, we have

\displaystyle\Phi(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s))\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\lesssim 1+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{6},

where we again used (
3.5
).
This, together with (
3.11
), implies

\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim 1+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{6}\mathrm{d}s.

We infer (
3.9
) by using the Bihari–Gronwall
inequality
(
Bihari, 1956
)
, completing the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

Assume that (
3.7
) holds for
s=1
.
Then for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha
satisfying (
2.5
), and
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]
, we have

(3.14)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}}^{2}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq c,

where
c
is independent of
n
, and
T^{\ast}
is defined by (
3.8
).

Proof.

Firstly, by (
A.4
) in the case
d=2
, and (
A.5
) in the case
d=3
, we have for any
\alpha
satisfying (
2.5
),

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}^{1+\alpha}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}^{-1+\alpha}}^{2}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}^{-1+\alpha}}^{2}

(3.15)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\lesssim 1.

Next, taking the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
norm of (
3.2
), then applying Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\kappa_{1}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\gamma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\kappa_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\kappa_{2}\mu\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{6}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}}^{2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{6}\lesssim 1,

where in the last step we used (
3.5
), Lemma
3.4
, and (
3.15
).

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\kappa_{1}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\gamma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\gamma\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}+\kappa_{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+3\kappa_{2}\mu\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}}^{2}\left(\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the penultimate step we used the embeddings
\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{W}^{1,4}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{\infty}
and
\mathbb{H}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{4}
, while in the last step we used (
3.15
) and Lemma
3.4
. Integrating both sides of the inequality over
(0,t)
and using Lemma
3.4
again then completes the proof.
∎

Altogether, the relevant results established in this section can be summarised as follows. Suppose
\alpha
satisfies (
2.5
).

•

If (
3.3
) holds, then we have:

(3.16)

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha})}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1}_{\Delta})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{4/3}_{T}(\mathbb{L}^{2})}\lesssim 1.

•

If (
3.7
) holds for
s=1
, then additionally we have the estimate:

(3.17)

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha})}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1}_{\Delta})}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{L}^{2})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}\lesssim 1,

where
T^{\ast}
is defined in (
3.8
).

•

Furthermore, it follows from the estimates established in Appendix
B
(summarised in (
B.11
)) that if (
3.7
) holds for
s=2
, then additionally we have

(3.18)

\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{L}^{2})}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1}_{\Delta})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha})}\lesssim 1.

We are now ready to prove the following result on existence and regularity of the solution.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
T>0
be fixed and let
T^{\ast}
be defined by (
3.8
).

(i)

If (
3.3
) holds, then there exists
a global weak solution
\boldsymbol{u}
to (
1.1
) in the sense of Definition
2.2
, satisfying

(3.19)

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha})}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1}_{\Delta})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{4/3}_{T}(\mathbb{L}^{2})}\leq c.

Moreover,
\boldsymbol{u}\in C^{0,\widetilde{\gamma}}([0,T];\mathbb{L}^{2})
for every
\widetilde{\gamma}\in[0,\frac{1}{4})
.

(ii)

If (
3.7
) holds for
s=1
, then there exists a strong solution to (
1.1
) in the sense of Definition
2.2
, which is global if
d=1,2
and local if
d=3
, satisfying

(3.20)

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha})}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1}_{\Delta})}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{L}^{2})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}\leq c.

Furthermore,
\boldsymbol{u}\in C^{0,\widetilde{\gamma}}([0,T^{\ast}];\mathbb{H}^{1})
for every
\widetilde{\gamma}\in[0,\frac{1}{2})
.

(iii)

If (
3.7
) holds for
s=2
, then there exists a strong solution to (
1.1
), which is global if
d=1,2
and local if
d=3
, satisfying

(3.21)

\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{L}^{2})}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1}_{\Delta})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha})}\lesssim 1.

Furthermore,
\boldsymbol{u}\in C^{0,\widetilde{\gamma}}([0,T^{\ast}];\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha})
for every
\widetilde{\gamma}\in[0,\frac{1}{2})
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}
. It follows from (
3.16
) that there exist

\boldsymbol{u}\in W^{1,4/3}_{T}(\mathbb{L}^{2})\cap L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1})\cap L^{2}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha})
and a subsequence
of
\{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, which we do not relabel, satisfying

(3.22)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\to\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle\text{weakly}^{\ast}\text{ in $W^{1,4/3}_{T}(\mathbb{L}^{2})$ as $n\to\infty$},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\to\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle\text{weakly}^{\ast}\text{ in $L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1})$ as $n\to\infty$},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\to\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle\text{weakly}\text{ in $L^{2}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha})$ as $n\to\infty$.}

Since the embeddings
\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{H}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{4}
are compact, it follows from the
Aubin–Lions lemma and (
5.18
) that

(3.23)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}-\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{L}^{4})}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}-\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}=0.

We now prove that
\boldsymbol{u}
satisfies (
2.6
) with
\epsilon=0
.
It follows from (
3.4a
) that for any test function

\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}
and for every
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s),\tilde{\Pi}_{n}\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s+\kappa_{1}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s),\nabla\tilde{\Pi}_{n}\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s

(3.24)

\displaystyle+\gamma\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s),\nabla\tilde{\Pi}_{n}\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s+\kappa_{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle(1+\mu|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)|^{2})\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s),\tilde{\Pi}_{n}\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s=0,

where
\tilde{\Pi}_{n}:\mathbb{H}^{1}\to\mathcal{S}_{n}
is the orthogonal projection with respect to the
\mathbb{H}^{1}
-inner product.
We recall that, for all
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|{\tilde{\Pi}_{n}\boldsymbol{\xi}-\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}=0.

Thus, it follows from (
3.22
)
that, when
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s),\tilde{\Pi}_{n}\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\to\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}(s),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s),\nabla\tilde{\Pi}_{n}\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\to\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(s),\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s.

For the first nonlinear term in (
3.24
) we have, by using the
triangle inequality and (
3.16
),

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s),\nabla\tilde{\Pi}_{n}\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s-\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}(s)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(s),\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left|\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s),\nabla(\tilde{\Pi}_{n}\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v})\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right|+\left|\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)-\boldsymbol{u}(s))\times\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s),\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right|

\displaystyle\qquad+\left|\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}(s)\times(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)-\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(s)),\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{L}^{\infty})}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{L}^{2})}\left\|{\nabla(\tilde{\Pi}_{n}\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}-\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{L}^{4})}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{L}^{4})}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{L}^{\infty})}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}-\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{L}^{2})}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\left\|{\nabla(\tilde{\Pi}_{n}\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}-\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{L}^{4})}+\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}-\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{L}^{2})}.

The right-hand side converges to zero due to the definition
of
\tilde{\Pi}
and (
3.23
), implying the convergence of this first
nonlinear term. Similar arguments can be used for the second nonlinear term
in (
3.24
). Therefore, by taking the limit when
n\to\infty
in
equation (
3.24
), we obtain that

\boldsymbol{u}
is a global weak solution. Taking the limit
n\to\infty
in (
3.16
) yields (
3.19
). This function
\boldsymbol{u}
admits a Hölder continous representative such that
\boldsymbol{u}\in C^{0,\widetilde{\gamma}}([0,T^{\ast}];\mathbb{L}^{2})
for every
\widetilde{\gamma}\in[0,\frac{1}{4})
by a version of vector-valued Morrey’s inequality
(
Arendt and Kreuter, 2018
)
.

Next, suppose
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}_{\Delta}
. We prove that
\boldsymbol{u}
satisfies (
2.7
) with
\epsilon=0
.
Since
\boldsymbol{u}
is a weak solution, we deduce from (
2.6
) with
\epsilon=0

and integration by parts that, for all
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}(t)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}+\kappa_{1}\int_{0}^{t}\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+\gamma\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{u}(s)\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s-\kappa_{2}\int_{0}^{t}(1+\mu|\boldsymbol{u}(s)|^{2})\boldsymbol{u}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\qquad\text{ in }\,\widetilde{\mathbb{H}}^{-1}.

We will show that the above equation holds in
\mathbb{L}^{2}
.
Indeed, inequality (
3.19
) implies that all the linear terms belong
to
\mathbb{L}^{2}
. For the nonlinear terms, by using Minkowski’s inequality, the
continuous embeddings
\mathbb{H}^{3/2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{\infty}
and

\mathbb{H}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{6}
, and (
3.22
), we deduce that
for any
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]

\displaystyle\left\|{\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{u}(s)\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\int_{0}^{t}(1+\mu|\boldsymbol{u}(s)|^{2})\boldsymbol{u}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(s)\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{(1+\mu|\boldsymbol{u}(s)|^{2})\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{L}^{\infty})}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{L}^{2})}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{1}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{L}^{2})}+\mu\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{3}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{L}^{6})}^{3}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{3/2})}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{L}^{2})}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{1}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{L}^{2})}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}^{3}<\infty.

This implies that the nonlinear terms also belong to
\mathbb{L}^{2}
and
therefore
\boldsymbol{u}
satisfies (
2.7
) with
\epsilon=0
. Estimate (
3.20
) follows by taking the limit
n\to\infty
in (
3.17
), thus showing that
\boldsymbol{u}
is a strong solution. This function
\boldsymbol{u}
admits a Hölder continous representative such that
\boldsymbol{u}\in C^{0,\widetilde{\gamma}}([0,T^{\ast}];\mathbb{H}^{1})
for every
\widetilde{\gamma}\in[0,\frac{1}{2})
by a version of vector-valued Morrey’s inequality
(
Arendt and Kreuter, 2018
)
.

Finally, if
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\Delta}
, then similar argument applies to (
3.18
), which yields (
3.21
). In this case,
\boldsymbol{u}
admits a Hölder continous representative such that
\boldsymbol{u}\in C^{0,\widetilde{\gamma}}([0,T^{\ast}];\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha})
for every
\widetilde{\gamma}\in[0,\frac{1}{2})
.
This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Next, we show a continuous dependence result for the solution of the LLBE with respect to the initial data, which implies uniqueness.

Theorem 3.7
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}_{\Delta}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{u}
and
\boldsymbol{v}
be the strong solutions
associated with the initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}

and
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}
, respectively, as conferred by
Theorem
3.6
. Then

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{L}^{2})}\lesssim\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{v}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},

where the constant may depend on
T^{\ast}
. This implies
uniqueness of the solution
\boldsymbol{u}
.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{w}_{0}:=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{v}_{0}

and
\boldsymbol{w}:=\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{v}
.
Then it follows from (
3.4a
) that, for any
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}
and
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{w}(t),\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\kappa_{1}\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{w}(t),\nabla\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{w}(t)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(t),\nabla\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}(t)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{w}(t),\nabla\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\kappa_{2}\left\langle\boldsymbol{w}(t),\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\kappa_{2}\mu\left\langle|\boldsymbol{u}(t)|^{2}\boldsymbol{w}(t)+(|\boldsymbol{u}(t)|^{2}-|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|^{2})\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}=0.

Putting
\boldsymbol{\phi}=2\boldsymbol{w}
in the above
and
integrating we obtain, for any
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+2\kappa_{1}\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{w}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s+2\kappa_{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{w}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s+2\kappa_{2}\mu\int_{0}^{t}\||\boldsymbol{u}(s)||\boldsymbol{w}(s)|\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-2\gamma\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\boldsymbol{w}(s)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(s),\nabla\boldsymbol{w}(s)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s-2\kappa_{2}\mu\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle(|\boldsymbol{u}(s)|^{2}-|\boldsymbol{v}(s)|^{2})\boldsymbol{v}(s),\boldsymbol{w}(s)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s.

The last two terms on the right-hand side can be estimated as
follows. For the middle term, by Lemma
A.3
we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\boldsymbol{w}(s)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(s),\nabla\boldsymbol{w}(s)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\Phi(\boldsymbol{u}(s))\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\delta\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

for any
\delta>0
, where
\Phi
is defined in (
A.6
).
For the last term, we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle(|\boldsymbol{u}(s)|^{2}-|\boldsymbol{v}(s)|^{2})\boldsymbol{v}(s),\boldsymbol{w}(s)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(s)+\boldsymbol{v}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(s)+\boldsymbol{v}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s+\delta\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

(3.25)

\displaystyle\leq c\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\delta\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

for any
\delta>0
, where in the penultimate step we used Young’s
inequality and the Sobolev embedding
\mathbb{H}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{6}
, and
in the last step we used (
3.20
).

Altogether, rearranging the terms and choosing
\delta>0
sufficiently small, we infer that

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\Phi(\boldsymbol{u}(s))\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s.

Note that
\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha})
for all
\alpha
satisfying (
2.5
) by (
3.20
). Thus, by the Sobolev embeddings
\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{W}^{1,2d}
for
d=1,2
and
\mathbb{H}^{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{W}^{1,6}
for
d=3
, we have

(3.26)

\int_{0}^{t}\Phi(\boldsymbol{u}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s\leq c.

Applying Gronwall’s inequality and
noting (
3.26
), we obtain the required result.
∎

As mentioned in the introduction, a remarkable property of the macroscopic magnetisation above the Curie
temperature is that it will spontaneously relax towards the zero state in the
long-run. In other words, the solution
\boldsymbol{u}(t)
to the LLBE
should converge to
\boldsymbol{0}
as
t\to\infty
. We conclude this section with a rigorous proof of this physical fact below.

Theorem 3.8
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}
be a strong solution to (
1.1
) and let
p\in[2,\infty]
. For all
t\in[0,\infty)
,

(3.27)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}\leq e^{-\kappa_{2}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}.

Proof.

First we consider
p\in[2,\infty)
. Putting
\boldsymbol{v}=|\boldsymbol{u}|^{p-2}\boldsymbol{u}
in (
3.1a
) and rearranging, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}+\kappa_{1}\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\nabla(\left|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right|^{p-2}\boldsymbol{u})\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\kappa_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}+\kappa_{2}\mu\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p+2}}^{p+2}=0,

Note that, for
q\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\nabla(\left|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right|^{q}\boldsymbol{u})

\displaystyle=\left|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right|^{q}\nabla\boldsymbol{u}+q\left|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right|^{q-2}\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}+\kappa_{1}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\kappa_{1}(p-2)\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{\frac{p-4}{2}}|\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\kappa_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}+\kappa_{2}\mu\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p+2}}^{p+2}=0,

which implies

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}+p\kappa_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}\leq 0,

so that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big(e^{p\kappa_{2}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{p}\Big)\leq 0.

Integrating and taking
p
-th root give

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}\leq e^{-\kappa_{2}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}.

This proves (
3.27
) for
p\in[2,\infty)
. Letting
p\to\infty
in
the above completes the proof for the case
p=\infty
.
∎

4.
Fully-discrete finite element approximation for the LLBE

In this section, we assume that
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\Delta}
. In particular, this guarantees a strong solution
\boldsymbol{u}
of the LLBE with regularity

(4.1)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}\in{W}^{1,\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1})\cap H^{1}_{T^{\ast}}(\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}).

exists (cf. Theorem
3.6
), where
\alpha
is defined in (
2.5
).

Let
\mathscr{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a domain satisfying the assumptions in Section
2.2
, and let
\{\mathcal{T}_{h}\}_{h>0}
be a family of quasi-uniform triangulations of
\mathscr{D}
with maximal mesh-size
h
. To discretise the LLBE, we introduce the Lagrange finite element space
\mathbb{V}_{h}\subset\mathbb{H}^{1}
, which is the space of all piecewise linear polynomials on
\mathcal{T}_{h}
.
Let
P_{h}:\mathbb{L}^{2}\to\mathbb{V}_{h}
be the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
-orthogonal projection onto
\mathbb{V}_{h}
. For any
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}
,
P_{h}\boldsymbol{v}
is defined by

(4.2)

\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}-P_{h}\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}=0,\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}.

Under the assumption of quasi-uniformity of the triangulations, we have the following stability
(
Crouzeix and Thomée, 1987
)
and approximation properties
(
Ern and Guermond, 2021
, Chapter 22)
of
P_{h}
, which we now state. Let
\alpha_{0}
be as defined in (
2.4
). Then for any
p\in[1,\infty]
and
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},\alpha_{0})
, there exist constants
c_{p}
and
c_{\alpha}
such that

(4.3)

\displaystyle\left\|{P_{h}\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{p}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}},\quad{\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{L}^{p}},

(4.4)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-P_{h}\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+h\left\|{\nabla(\boldsymbol{v}-P_{h}\boldsymbol{v})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\alpha}h^{1+\alpha}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}},\quad{\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}}.

If
\mathscr{D}
is a convex polytopal domain, then (
4.4
) also holds for
\alpha=1
.

We also need a well-known inverse estimate. Let
\ell_{h}
be defined by

(4.5)

\ell_{h}:=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }d=1,\\
\left|{\log h}\right|^{1/2},&\text{if }d=2,\\
h^{-1/2},&\text{if }d=3.\end{cases}

If the family of triangulations
\{\mathcal{T}_{h}\}_{h>0}
is quasi-uniform, then there exists a constant
c_{\mathrm{i}}
(dependent on the regularity of the triangulation, but independent of
h
) such that for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
,

(4.6)

\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\leq c_{\mathrm{i}}\ell_{h}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}.

We now propose a finite element scheme for the LLBE and
provide error estimates for the approximate solution. To discretise in time, let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
k=T/N
. We
partition
[0,T]
into
N
uniform subintervals with nodes
t_{n}=kn
for
n=0,1,2,\ldots,N
. For a time-discrete function
\boldsymbol{v}^{(j)}
, define
\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{v}^{(j)}:=(\boldsymbol{v}^{(j)}-\boldsymbol{v}^{(j-1)})/k
.

Algorithm 4.1

(Linear FEM for the LLBE)
.

Let
h>0
and
k>0
be given.

Input
: Given
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(0)}=P_{h}\boldsymbol{u}(0)\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
.

For

j=1
to
N

do
: Find
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}
such that

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)},\boldsymbol{\phi}_{h}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle+\kappa_{1}\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)},\nabla\boldsymbol{\phi}_{h}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j-1)}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)},\nabla\boldsymbol{\phi}_{h}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

(4.7)

\displaystyle\quad+\kappa_{2}\mu\left\langle|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j-1)}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)},\boldsymbol{\phi}_{h}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\kappa_{2}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{j},\boldsymbol{\phi}_{h}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}=0,\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\phi}_{h}\in\mathbb{V}_{h}.

Output
: a sequence of finite element functions
\big\{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)}\big\}_{j=1}^{N}
.

Algorithm
4.1
is well-posed by the Lax–Milgram theorem. Stability of the finite element solution in
\ell^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{2})\cap\ell^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{1})
,
which we state below, holds unconditionally for an arbitrary number of iterations
n
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\big\{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)}\big\}_{j=1}^{N}
be the sequence defined by Algorithm
4.1
. For any
n\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
, there holds

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(n)}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+2k\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j-1)}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(0)}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{h}=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)}
in equation (
4.7
). Noting the vector identity

(4.8)

2\boldsymbol{a}\cdot(\boldsymbol{a}-\boldsymbol{b})=\left|{\boldsymbol{a}}\right|^{2}-\left|{\boldsymbol{b}}\right|^{2}+\left|{\boldsymbol{a}-\boldsymbol{b}}\right|^{2},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{3},

we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\kappa_{1}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

(4.9)

\displaystyle+k\kappa_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\kappa_{2}\mu\left\|{\big|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j-1)}\big|\big|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)}\big|}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Summing over
j=1,2,\ldots,n
yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(n)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\kappa_{1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\kappa_{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle+k\kappa_{2}\mu\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\|{\big|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j-1)}\big|\big|\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j)}\big|}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(0)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

as required.
∎

The following estimates on certain nonlinear terms will be needed in the proof of the main theorem.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{v}^{(j)}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
, be a (finite) sequence of functions
in
\mathbb{H}^{1}\cap\mathbb{L}^{\infty}
and
\boldsymbol{w}
be a function in
L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha})\cap W^{1,\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1})
. Assume that we can write

\boldsymbol{v}^{(j)}-\boldsymbol{w}(t_{j})=\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(j)}+\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)}.

Define, for
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}
,

\displaystyle H_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}^{(j)},\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{\phi})

\displaystyle:=k\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}^{(j-1)}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{v}^{(j)},\nabla\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\int_{t_{j-1}}^{t_{j}}\left\langle\boldsymbol{w}(s)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{w}(s),\nabla\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle H_{2}(\boldsymbol{v}^{(j)},\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{\phi})

\displaystyle:=k\left\langle|\boldsymbol{v}^{(j-1)}|^{2}\boldsymbol{w}(t_{j}),\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\int_{t_{j-1}}^{t_{j}}\left\langle|\boldsymbol{w}(s)|^{2}\boldsymbol{w}(s),\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s.

Then, for any
\delta>0
, there exists a positive constant
c

depending on
\delta
but independent of
k
and
j
such that

\displaystyle\big|H_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}^{(j)},\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)})\big|

\displaystyle\leq ck\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}^{(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+ck\Big(\left\|{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\Big)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha})}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+ck^{3}\Big(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}^{(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha})}^{2}\Big)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}^{2}

(4.10)

\displaystyle\quad+\delta k\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha})}^{2}+4\delta k\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

and

\displaystyle|H_{2}(\boldsymbol{v}^{(j)},\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)})|

\displaystyle\leq ck\left\|{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(j-1)}+\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}^{(j-1)}+\boldsymbol{w}(t_{j-1})}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+ck^{3}\Big(\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{L}^{2})}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}^{(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}^{2}\Big)

(4.11)

\displaystyle\quad+2\delta k\left\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\delta k\left\|{|\boldsymbol{v}^{(j-1)}||\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

provided that all the norms on the right-hand sides are well defined.

Furthermore, if
\mathscr{D}
is
convex
and
\boldsymbol{w}\in L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{W}^{2,3})\cap W^{1,\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
, then

\displaystyle\big|H_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}^{(j)},\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)})\big|

\displaystyle\leq ck\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}^{(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}^{2}+ck\Big(\left\|{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\Big)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+ck^{3}\Big(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}^{(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}\Big)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}

(4.12)

\displaystyle\quad+\delta k\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}+4\delta k\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Proof.

We first write
H_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}^{(j)},\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)})
as:

\displaystyle H_{1}(\boldsymbol{v}^{(j)},\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)})

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{j-1}}^{t_{j}}\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}^{(j-1)}\times(\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(j)}+\nabla\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)}),\nabla\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t_{j-1}}^{t_{j}}\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}^{(j-1)}\times(\nabla\boldsymbol{w}(t_{j})-\nabla\boldsymbol{w}(s)),\nabla\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t_{j-1}}^{t_{j}}\left\langle\big(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(j-1)}+\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j-1)}\big)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{w}(s),\nabla\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t_{j-1}}^{t_{j}}\left\langle\big(\boldsymbol{w}(t_{j-1})-\boldsymbol{w}(s)\big)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{w}(s),\nabla\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s

(4.13)

\displaystyle=:T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}+T_{4}.

We now estimate each term on the right-hand side of (
4.13
). Firstly, by using
Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities we have for the first term

\displaystyle|T_{1}|

\displaystyle:=\Big|\int_{t_{j-1}}^{t_{j}}\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}^{(j-1)}\times(\nabla\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(j)}+\nabla\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)}),\nabla\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\Big|
