\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\Delta}
in domains where
\mathbb{W}^{2,3}
-elliptic regularity result holds, for instance in rectangular parallelepipeds
(
Dauge, 1992
)
, or more generally in convex polyhedral domains satisfying certain interior angle restrictions (
(
Grisvard, 2011
, Chapter 4)
.

Theorem 6.4
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{D}
is a
convex
polytopal domain. Assume that
\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}
is the strong solution of the
\epsilon
-LLBE with higher regularity:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}\in L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{W}^{2,3})\cap W^{1,\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{2}).

Let
\big\{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(j)}\big\}_{j=1}^{N}
be the sequence generated by Algorithm
6.1
.
Then for any
n=1,\ldots,N
, where
t_{n}\in[0,T]
, we have, unconditionally,

(6.14)

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(n)}-\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t_{n})}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\leq ce^{cT}(h+k),

where the positive constant
c
is independent of
n
,
h
, or
k
, but may depend on
\epsilon^{-1}
.

Proof.

The proof proceeds in the same manner as that of Theorem
6.3
. However, instead of using (
4.10
) to obtain (
6.12
), we now apply (
4.12
) to infer that

\displaystyle R_{3}

\displaystyle\leq ck\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\epsilon,(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}^{2}+ck\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\epsilon,(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}+ck^{3}

\displaystyle\quad+\delta k\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+4\delta k\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq ck(h^{2}+k^{2})+ck\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\delta k\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+4\delta k\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the last step we have used the estimate
(
Crouzeix and Thomée, 1987
)
:

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\epsilon,(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}\leq ch\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{W}^{2,3})}.

The rest of the proof proceeds in the same manner as before, leading to (
6.14
).
∎

Remark 6.5
.

As a result of Theorem
5.7
and either Theorem
6.3
or Theorem
6.4
, an approximate solution
\boldsymbol{u}_{h,k}^{\epsilon}
can be defined such that, for
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h,k}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h,k}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}+c\epsilon+c(\epsilon)e^{c(\epsilon)T}(h+k),

where
c(\epsilon)
is a constant depending on
\epsilon^{-1}
.
Therefore, convergence of the numerical scheme is to be understood as

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\lim_{h,k\to 0^{+}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h,k}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathbb{H}^{1})}=0.

7.
Uniform-in-time error estimates for the FEM approximation of the
\epsilon
-LLBE

The result in Theorem
6.3
suggests that the
approximation error grows exponentially with the final time
T
. As such,
estimate (
6.4
) will not be useful for assessing the approximation
error in the long run.
The aim of this section is to better approximate the long-run trajectory of the
solution and derive error estimates for the approximation of the
\epsilon
-LLBE
that are uniform in time. Similar results are studied for the Navier–Stokes
equation
(
Heywood and Rannacher, 1986
)
, a semilinear parabolic equation
(
Larsson, 1989
)
, and the
parabolic
p
-Laplacian equation
(
Ju, 2000
)
, to name a few. To this end, we
need to derive decay estimates on the solution
\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}
and its approximation
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(n)}
for time
t\in[\hat{t},\infty)
. Here,

\hat{t}
denotes a sufficiently large time, which depends on the coefficients
of the equation.

Since we are interested in small values of
\epsilon
, we also assume that

\epsilon<\kappa_{1}/\kappa_{2}
for simplicity of presentation. Similar decay
estimate would still hold for bigger values of
\epsilon
. We assume in this section that a strong solution corresponding to an initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\Delta}
exists, and thus instead of working with the
Faedo–Galerkin solution
\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}
, we can work directly with

\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Suppose
\epsilon<\kappa_{1}/\kappa_{2}
and let
\alpha
satisfies (
2.5
).

(i)

For every
t\in[0,\infty)
,

(7.1)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq ce^{-2\kappa_{2}t},

(7.2)

\displaystyle\epsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq ce^{-2\kappa_{2}t},

where the constant
c
depends on

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}_{\Delta}}
, but is independent
of
\epsilon
and
t
.

(ii)

There exists
\hat{t}
depending on the coefficients
in (
1.2a
) such that for
t\in[\hat{t},\infty)
,

(7.3)

\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq ce^{-\kappa_{2}t},

where
c
depends on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}_{\Delta}}
and

\hat{t}
, but is independent of
t
.

(iii)

Furthermore, if
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\Delta}
, then there
exists
\hat{t}
depending on the coefficients of the
equation such that for
t\in[\hat{t},\infty)
,

(7.4)

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq ce^{-\kappa_{2}t},

(7.5)

\displaystyle\epsilon\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq ce^{-\kappa_{2}t},

where
c
depends on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\Delta}}
and

\hat{t}
, but is
independent of
t
.

Proof.

Define

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}(t)

\displaystyle:=\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},\qquad

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(t)

\displaystyle:=\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(t)

\displaystyle:=\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},\qquad

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}(t)

\displaystyle:=\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Proof of (i)
:
Repeating the arguments in the proof of Lemma
5.1
,
considering (
2.6
) instead of (
5.4a
),
we obtain exactly the same equations as (
5.6
)
and (
5.7
) with
\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}
replaced
by
\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}
. Consequently,

(7.6)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Psi_{0}(t)+2\kappa_{2}\Psi_{0}(t)\leq 0\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Psi_{1}(t)+2\kappa_{2}\Psi_{1}(t)\leq 0,

resulting in

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}(t)\leq\Psi_{0}(0)\cdot e^{-2\kappa_{2}t}\quad\text{and}\quad\Psi_{1}(t)\leq\Psi_{1}(0)\cdot e^{-2\kappa_{2}t}.

Estimate (
7.1
) then follows. Estimate (
7.2
) follows by the same argument as in (
5.13
).

Proof of (ii)
: Repeating the arguments in the proof of
Lemma
B.1
, we obtain analogously to (
B.3
)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Psi_{2}(t)

\displaystyle+2\kappa_{2}\Psi_{2}(t)+(2\kappa_{1}-2\kappa_{2}\epsilon)\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\gamma\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{2}\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{2\gamma^{2}}{\kappa_{2}\epsilon}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{2}\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{2\gamma^{2}}{\kappa_{2}\epsilon^{2}}\epsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}}^{2}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{2}\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{2c_{0}\gamma^{2}}{\kappa_{2}\epsilon^{2}}e^{-2\kappa_{2}t}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2},

where in the penultimate step we used the
embedding
\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{W}^{1,4}
and in the last step we
used (
7.2
) with the constant
c
distinguished by
c_{0}
.
It follows from the Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality and Young’s
inequality that

\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\leq c\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{1-d/4}\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{d/4}\leq c\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\kappa_{2}\epsilon^{2}}{2c_{0}\gamma^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Psi_{2}(t)+2\kappa_{2}\Psi_{2}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{2}\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{2cc_{0}\gamma^{2}}{\kappa_{2}\epsilon^{2}}e^{-2\kappa_{2}t}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+e^{-2\kappa_{2}t}\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Choose
\hat{t}
sufficiently large such that

\frac{2cc_{0}\gamma^{2}}{\kappa_{2}\epsilon^{2}}e^{-2\kappa_{2}\hat{t}}\leq\kappa_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad e^{-2\kappa_{2}\hat{t}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{2}\epsilon.

Then for
t\geq\hat{t}
, we deduce

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Psi_{2}(t)+\kappa_{2}\Psi_{2}(t)\leq 0,

which then yields (
7.3
).

Proof of (iii)
:
Similarly to the proof of (i) and (ii), we follow the argument leading
to (
3.10
) (or (
B.8
)) to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Psi_{3}(t)

\displaystyle+2\kappa_{2}\Psi_{3}(t)+(2\kappa_{1}-2\kappa_{2}\epsilon)\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\gamma\left|\left\langle\nabla\Big(\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)\Big),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\right|+2\kappa_{2}\mu\left|\left\langle\nabla\Big(|\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)\Big),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle=:R_{1}+R_{2}.

For the term
R_{1}
, by using successively Hölder’s inequality, the Sobolev
embedding
\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{W}^{1,4}
, Young’s inequality, and (
7.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle=2\gamma\left|\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq c\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\epsilon}e^{-2\kappa_{2}t}\big(\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\big)\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\epsilon}e^{-2\kappa_{2}t}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{c}{\epsilon}e^{-\kappa_{2}t}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

By similar argument, using the embedding
\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{\infty}
,

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle\leq c\left|\left\langle(\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t))\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\right|+c\left|\left\langle|\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)|^{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq c\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq c\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}}^{2}\Big(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\Big)\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\epsilon}e^{-2\kappa_{2}t}\Big(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\Big)\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\epsilon}e^{-2\kappa_{2}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{c}{\epsilon}e^{-2\kappa_{2}t}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{c}{\epsilon}e^{-2\kappa_{2}t}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Altogether, we have

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Psi_{3}(t)+2\kappa_{2}\Psi_{3}(t)\leq\frac{c}{\epsilon}e^{-2\kappa_{2}t}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right).

By choosing
\hat{t}
sufficiently large so that

\frac{c}{\epsilon}e^{-2\kappa_{2}t}\leq\kappa_{2}\epsilon,

upon rearranging the terms we deduce

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Psi_{3}(t)+\kappa_{2}\Psi_{3}(t)\leq\kappa_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\quad\forall t\in[\hat{t},\infty),

which together with (
7.6
) yields

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big(\Psi_{0}(t)+\Psi_{3}(t)\Big)+\kappa_{2}\Big(\Psi_{0}(t)+\Psi_{3}(t)\Big)\leq 0\quad\forall t\in[\hat{t},\infty).

This implies

\Psi_{0}(t)+\Psi_{3}(t)\leq e^{-\kappa_{2}t}\quad\forall t\in[\hat{t},\infty),

which yields (
7.4
).

Finally, it follows from (
1.2a
) that

\displaystyle\epsilon\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\kappa_{1}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\gamma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\kappa_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\kappa_{2}\mu\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{6}\leq ce^{-\kappa_{2}t},

where we used (
7.2
), (
7.3
), and (
7.4
) in the last step. By the same argument as in the proof of (
7.2
), we obtain (
7.5
).
∎

In the following lemma, we derive a decay estimate for the finite element
approximation of the solution
\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}
.

Lemma 7.2
.

Assume that
\epsilon<\kappa_{1}/\kappa_{2}
. For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(n)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(n)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq\Big(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(0)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(0)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\Big)e^{-\lambda t_{n}},

where
\lambda=2\kappa_{2}(1+2\kappa_{2}k)^{-1}
. In particular,
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(n)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(n)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\to 0
as
t_{n}\to\infty
.

Proof.

From (
4.9
), writing
a_{j}:=\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{a_{j}-a_{j-1}}{k}+2\kappa_{2}a_{j}+(2\kappa_{1}-2\kappa_{2}\epsilon)\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq 0.

The result then follows from a discrete version of Gronwall’s inequality

(
Emmrich, 1999
, Proposition 3.1)
.
∎

We will exploit these exponential decay estimates to obtain an error estimate
that does not deteriorate over a long time
t\in[\hat{t},\infty)
. First, note
that as a consequence of (
4.16
),
(
7.1
)–(
7.5
), and the assumed regularity of
\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}
, we
have, for
t\in[\hat{t},\infty)
,

(7.7)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\rho}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+h^{\alpha}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\rho}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq ch^{1+\alpha}e^{-\kappa_{2}t},

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\rho}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+h^{\alpha}\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\rho}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq ch^{1+\alpha}e^{-\kappa_{2}t}.

Furthermore, noting the embeddings
\mathbb{H}^{1+\alpha}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{W}^{1,4}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{\infty}
, inequality (
4.18
) becomes

(7.8)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(s_{2})-\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(s_{1})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}

\displaystyle\leq cke^{-\frac{\kappa_{2}s_{1}}{2}}\quad\forall p\in[1,\infty],

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(s_{2})-\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(s_{1})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}

\displaystyle\leq cke^{-\frac{\kappa_{2}s_{1}}{2}}\quad\forall p\in[1,4],

for any
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
satisfying
\hat{t}\leq t_{j-1}\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq t_{j}
for
some
j=1,\ldots,N
.

We are now ready to prove the main result of this section.

Theorem 7.3
.

Suppose that
\epsilon<\kappa_{1}/\kappa_{2}
. Assume that the strong solution
\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}
of the
\epsilon
-LLBE corresponding to initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\Delta}
exists, satisfying the decay estimates in Lemma
7.1
. Assume that
k\ell_{h}\lesssim 1
, where
\ell_{h}
is defined in (
4.5
). For any
n=1,\ldots,N
, where
t_{n}\in[0,T]
, and
\alpha
satisfying (
2.5
), we have

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(n)}-\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}(t_{n})}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\leq c(h^{\alpha}+k),

where
c
is a constant independent of
n
,
h
,
k
, and
T
(but may depend on
\epsilon
).

Proof.

Repeating the arguments in the proof of Theorem
6.3
, but
replacing (
4.16
) and (
4.18
) by (
7.7
) and (
7.8
), respectively, we obtain, similarly
to (
6.13
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\left(\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j)}-\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\kappa_{1}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\kappa_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+k\kappa_{2}\mu\left\|{\left|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(j-1)}}\right|\left|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j)}}\right|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c(1+M)k(h^{2\alpha}+k^{2})e^{-ct_{j-1}}+cke^{-ct_{j-1}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\delta k\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\delta k\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j-1)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\delta k\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\delta k\left\|{\left|{\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(j-1)}}\right|\left|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j)}}\right|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where
M
is defined in (
6.8
).
We now choose
\delta<\frac{1}{8}\min\{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{2}\mu\}
, and let
j\geq j_{0}
with

j_{0}
sufficiently large so that
ce^{-ct_{j-1}}<\kappa_{2}/4
. In this manner, the last three terms on the right-hand
side can be absorbed to corresponding terms on the left-hand side.
Putting

a_{j}:=(1+k\kappa_{2})\left\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+(\epsilon+k\kappa_{1})\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(j)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

we deduce

a_{j}-a_{j-1}\leq c(1+M)k(h^{2\alpha}+k^{2})e^{-ct_{j-1}},\quad\forall j\geq j_{0}.

Summing over
j
from
j_{0}
to
n
yields

\displaystyle a_{n}

\displaystyle\leq a_{j_{0}-1}+c(1+M)k(h^{2\alpha}+k^{2})\sum_{j=j_{0}}^{\infty}e^{-ct_{j-1}}

\displaystyle\leq ce^{cj_{0}k}(h^{2\alpha}+k^{2})+ck(h^{2\alpha}+k^{2})\sum_{j=j_{0}}^{\infty}e^{-c(j-1)k},

where in the last step we used Theorem
6.3
.
The constant
c
depends only on
the coefficients of the equation. Since
k\sum_{j=j_{0}}^{\infty}e^{-c(j-1)k}
is bounded by a constant depending on
j_{0}
, we obtain the
required estimate for
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\epsilon,(n)}
. The required result then
follows by induction and the triangle inequality, similar to the proof of Theorem
6.3
.
∎

8.
Numerical simulations

To assess convergence, we perform several simulations using the open-source package
FEniCS

(
Alnaes et al., 2015
)
. The results are presented in this section. Recall that
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(n)}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(n)}
are computed by Algorithm
4.1
and Algorithm
6.1
, respectively. We carry out four numerical observations to assess the convergence with respect to
h
,
k
, and
\epsilon
separately:

(O1)

Snapshots at selected times: We run the simulation with a fixed
\epsilon=0.001
,
h=1/8
, and
k=2.5\times 10^{-3}
to obtain snapshots of the magnetic spin field
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(n)}
at selected times (i.e., selected values of
n
).

(O2)

Convergence with respect to
h
:
Since the exact solution of the equation is not known, we use extrapolation to verify the order of convergence experimentally. We fix
\epsilon=0.001
and
k=2.5\times 10^{-3}
, then vary
h
. For
s=0
or
1
, the extrapolated spatial orders of convergence are computed by

(8.1)

h\text{-rate}_{s}:=\log_{2}\left[\frac{\max_{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}_{2h}^{(n)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}}{\max_{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}_{h}^{(n)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}}\right]\quad\text{and}\quad h\text{-rate}_{s}^{\epsilon}:=\log_{2}\left[\frac{\max_{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}_{2h}^{\epsilon,(n)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}}{\max_{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}_{h}^{\epsilon,(n)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}}\right],

where

\boldsymbol{e}_{h}^{(n)}:=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(n)}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h/2}^{(n)}
and
\boldsymbol{e}_{h}^{\epsilon,(n)}:=\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(n)}-\boldsymbol{u}_{h/2}^{\epsilon,(n)}
, depending on the context. We perform log-log plots of
\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}_{h}^{(n)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}
(or
\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}_{h}^{\epsilon,(n)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}
) against
1/h
. It is expected that
h\text{-rate}_{s}\approx 2-s
and
h\text{-rate}_{s}^{\epsilon}\approx 2-s
(Theorems
6.3
and
6.4
).

(O3)

Convergence with respect to
k
:
The temporal order of convergence is verified by comparing numerical solutions against a reference solution
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathrm{ref}}(T)
of the LLBE (or
\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{\mathrm{ref}}(T)
of the
\epsilon
-LLBE), computed with a significantly refined time step. We fix the spatial resolution, while varying
k=T/(10\times 2^{j})
, where
j=0,1,\ldots,4
. Let
\boldsymbol{f}_{k}^{(N)}=\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathrm{ref}}(T)-\boldsymbol{u}_{h,k}^{(N)}
and
\boldsymbol{f}_{k}^{\epsilon,(N)}=\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathrm{ref}}^{\epsilon}(T)-\boldsymbol{u}_{h,k}^{\epsilon,(N)}
be the errors at the fixed final time
T
. We perform log-log plots of
\left\|{\boldsymbol{f}_{k}^{(N)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}
(or
\left\|{\boldsymbol{f}_{k}^{\epsilon,(N)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}
) against
1/k
. The temporal convergence rate is observed by computing

(8.2)

k\text{-rate}_{s}:=\log_{2}\left[\frac{\left\|{\boldsymbol{f}_{2k}^{(N)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}}{\left\|{\boldsymbol{f}_{k}^{(N)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}}\right]\quad\text{and}\quad k\text{-rate}^{\epsilon}_{s}:=\log_{2}\left[\frac{\left\|{\boldsymbol{f}_{2k}^{\epsilon,(N)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}}{\left\|{\boldsymbol{f}_{k}^{\epsilon,(N)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}}\right],\quad s=0,1.

It is expected that
k\text{-rate}_{s}\approx 1
(Theorem
4.4
) and
k\text{-rate}^{\epsilon}_{s}\approx 1
(Theorems
6.3
and
6.4
) for
s=0,1
.

(O4)

Convergence with respect to
\epsilon
: The convergence with respect to
\epsilon
is assessed by comparing numerical solutions
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(N)}
against a reference solution
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathrm{ref}}(T):=\boldsymbol{u}_{h_{0}}^{(N)}
, where
h_{0}
and the time step are chosen to be sufficiently small, the values of which will be specified in each simulation. We vary the parameter
\epsilon
through successive halving, while keeping
h
and
k
fixed. Let
\boldsymbol{g}^{\epsilon,(N)}:=\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathrm{ref}}(T)-\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(N)}
. A log-log plot of
\left\|{\boldsymbol{g}^{\epsilon,(N)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}
against
1/\epsilon
is performed. The convergence rate with respect to
\epsilon
is assessed by computing

(8.3)

\epsilon\text{-rate}_{s}:=\log_{2}\left[\frac{\left\|{\boldsymbol{g}^{2\epsilon,(N)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}}{\left\|{\boldsymbol{g}^{\epsilon,(N)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}}\right],\quad s=0,1.

It is expected that
\epsilon\text{-rate}_{s}\approx 1
(Theorem
5.7
) for
s=0,1
.

The details of observations (O1)–(O4) are provided within the description of each simulation below.

8.1.
Simulation 1: Square domain

We fix the domain
\mathscr{D}:=[0,1]^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The coefficients in (
1.1
) are taken to be
\kappa_{1}=5.0,\kappa_{2}=2.0,\mu=1.0
, and
\gamma=50.0
. The initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
is given by

\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(x,y)=\big(\cos(2\pi x),\,\sin(2\pi y),\,2\cos(2\pi x)\sin(2\pi y)\big).

We carry out four numerical observations as explained above:

(O1)

First, we run the simulation using Algorithm
6.1
as shown in Figure
1
. In this configuration, one could see the formation of a Bloch wall around time
t=0.2
, which is dissipating as time progresses. Eventually, the magnetisation vectors will decay to
\boldsymbol{0}
as
t\to\infty
. The plots showing the decay of
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(n)}
in the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
,
\mathbb{L}^{\infty}
, and
\mathbb{H}^{1}
-norms are shown in Figures
2(a)
and
2(b)
.

(O2)

We perform several numerical simulations with various values of
h
to compute
\boldsymbol{e}_{h}^{\epsilon,(n)}
. The plot of
\max_{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}_{h}^{\epsilon,(n)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}
against
1/h
(with
h=2^{-j}
, where
j=2,3,\ldots,7
) to observe
h\text{-rate}_{s}^{\epsilon}
(defined in (
8.1
)) is shown in Figure
3(a)
, confirming the spatial order of convergence.

(O3)

To assess the temporal order of convergence, we set
T=0.2
and compute the reference solution
\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{\mathrm{ref}}(T)
with
h=1/64
and
k=T/640
. The plot of
\left\|{\boldsymbol{f}_{k}^{\epsilon,(N)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}
against
1/k
(with
k=T/(10\times 2^{j})
, where
j=0,1,\ldots,4
) to observe
k\text{-rate}_{s}^{\epsilon}
(defined in (
8.2
)) is shown in Figure
3(b)
.

The same experiments as above are carried out with
\epsilon=0
, i.e., we use Algorithm
4.1
to compute
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{(n)}
. The corresponding results are presented in Figures
4
,
5(a)
, and
5(b)
. We see that the order of convergence does not deteriorate compared to the previous simulation with
\epsilon>0
, indicating that the order of convergence in Theorem
4.4
may be sub-optimal. Qualitatively, the solution
\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}
in Figure
1
and the solution
\boldsymbol{u}
in Figure
4
look similar.

(O4)

Finally, to observe
\epsilon\text{-rate}_{s}
, we compute the reference solution
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathrm{ref}}(T)
at
T=0.2
with
\epsilon=0
,
h=1/64
, and
k=2\times 10^{-3}
. Subsequently, with
h=1/16
and
k=2\times 10^{-2}
, we vary
\epsilon=2^{-j}
, for
j=3,4,\ldots,9
, to compute the corresponding solutions
\boldsymbol{u}_{h}^{\epsilon,(N)}
, and plot
\left\|{\boldsymbol{g}^{\epsilon,(N)}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}
against
1/\epsilon
. This last procedure is repeated with
h=1/64
and
k=2\times 10^{-3}
to demonstrate how the mesh size and time step influence the
\epsilon
-convergence. The resulting convergence behaviour as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
is displayed in Figure
6
.

8.3.
Simulation 3: L-shaped domain

We set
\mathscr{D}:=[-1,1]^{2}\setminus[0,1]^{2}\subset{R}^{2}
, which is a non-convex domain. The coefficients in (
1.1
) are taken to be
\kappa_{1}=0.5,\kappa_{2}=2.0,\mu=1.0
, and
\gamma=50.0
. The initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
is

\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(x,y)=\big(2x^{2},\,2y,\,x^{2}-2y^{2}\big).

Four numerical observations are carried out as before:

(O1)

The result of this simulation is displayed in Figure
10
.

(O2)

The log-log plot with
h=2^{-j}
, where
j=2,3,\ldots,7
, is shown in Figure
11(a)
to observe
h\text{-rate}_{s}^{\epsilon}
. As seen here, the singularity at the non-convex corner pollutes the finite element solution everywhere in
\mathscr{D}
, leading to
O(h^{\alpha})
and
O(h^{2\alpha})
order of convergence in
{H}^{1}
and
{L}^{2}
norms, respectively, for every
\alpha<\alpha_{0}
. For an L-shaped domain,
\alpha_{0}=2/3
; see (
2.4
).

(O3)

To verify
k\text{-rate}^{\epsilon}_{s}
, we set
T=0.1
and compute the reference solution
\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{\mathrm{ref}}(T)
with
h=1/64
and
k=T/640
. The log-log plot with
k=T/(10\times 2^{j})
, where
j=0,1,\ldots,4
, is shown in Figure
11(b)
.

(O4)

To observe
\epsilon\text{-rate}_{s}
, we compute
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathrm{ref}}(T)
at
T=0.1
with
\epsilon=0
,
h=1/128
, and
k=10^{-3}
. Next, with
h=1/64
,
k=2.5\times 10^{-3}
, we compute
\left\|{\boldsymbol{g}^{\epsilon,(N)}}\right\|_{{H}^{s}}
with
\epsilon=2^{-j}
, for
j=3,4,\ldots,8
. This procedure is repeated with
h=1/128
and
k=10^{-3}
. The log-log plot is displayed in Figure
12
.

(a)

t=0

(b)

Appendix A
Auxiliary results

Some auxiliary results which are needed in the analysis are collected in this section.
Firstly, if
\boldsymbol{u}\in{H}^{1}_{\Delta}\cap{H}^{1+\alpha}
and
\boldsymbol{v}\in{H}^{\alpha}
(where
\alpha
satisfies (
2.5
)),
and
\partial\boldsymbol{u}/\partial\boldsymbol{\nu}=0
on
\partial\mathscr{D}
in the sense of trace (where
\boldsymbol{\nu}
is the outward normal vector
to
\partial\mathscr{D}
), then we can use integration-by-parts formula to obtain

(A.1)

\displaystyle-\left\langle\Delta\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{3}\left\langle\Delta u_{i},v_{i}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\left\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla v_{i}\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}=\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}.

Moreover, if
\boldsymbol{v}\in{H}^{1+\alpha}
, then by using the well-known fact
(
Brezis and Mironescu, 2001
)
that
{H}^{s}(\mathscr{D})
is an algebra under multiplication if
s>d/2
, we have
\boldsymbol{v}\times\boldsymbol{u}\in{H}^{1+\alpha}
. Hence from (
A.1
), we infer

(A.2)

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\Delta\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\times\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:vec Gre 1}}}{{=}}-\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\nabla(\boldsymbol{v}\times\boldsymbol{u})\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}=-\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}.

For a bounded Lipschitz domain, certain embedding theorems and the Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequalities hold (see
(
Brezis and Mironescu, 2018
;
Brezis and Mironescu, 2019
;
Di Nezza et al., 2012
)
), which we will use extensively in the analysis.

Next, we gather some elliptic regularity (and the associated regularity shift) theorems for bounded polytopal domains. For a polygonal domain, refer to
(
Dauge, 1992
;
Li, 2022
)
. For a polyhedral domain, see
(
Dauge, 1992
;
Jerison and Kenig, 1989
)
.

Theorem A.1

(two-dimensional polygonal domains)
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{D}
is a bounded polygonal domain which has
m
re-entrant corners with opening angles
\omega_{j}\in(\pi,2\pi)
, for
j=1,2,\ldots,m
. Let
\eta_{j}=\pi/\omega_{j}
so that
\eta_{j}\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
, and let
\eta_{0}:=\min_{j}\eta_{j}
. Let
\boldsymbol{f}\in\widetilde{{H}}^{-1+\eta_{0}}
be given. Then for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
, there exists a unique weak solution
\boldsymbol{v}\in{H}^{1+\eta}
to the elliptic boundary value problem

(A.3a)

\displaystyle-\Delta\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{f}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }\mathscr{D},

(A.3b)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\nu}}

\displaystyle=0\qquad

\displaystyle\text{on }\partial\mathscr{D},

satisfying the regularity shift estimate

(A.4)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{H}^{1+\eta}}\leq c\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\widetilde{{H}}^{-1+\eta}}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\widetilde{{H}}^{-1+\eta}}\right).

Theorem A.2

(convex polyhedral domain)
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{D}
is a
convex
polyhedral domain. If
\boldsymbol{f}\in{L}^{p}
for some
p\in(1,2]
, then there exists a unique weak solution
\boldsymbol{v}\in{W}^{2,p}
to the same problem, satisfying

(A.5)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{W}^{2,p}}\leq c\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{p}}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{p}}\right).

Here, the constant
c
depends only on the domain
\mathscr{D}
.

We also need the following technical lemma.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
d
be the dimension of the domain
\mathscr{D}
. For any
\boldsymbol{v}\in{H}^{1}
,
\boldsymbol{w}\in{W}^{1,2d}
, and
\delta>0
, the
following inequality holds:

\Big|\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{w},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}\Big|\leq\Phi(\boldsymbol{w})\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\delta\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

where

(A.6)

\Phi(\boldsymbol{w}):=\begin{cases}\frac{c^{2}}{\delta}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{c^{2}}{4\delta^{3}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{4},\quad&d=1,\\[4.30554pt]
\frac{3^{3}c^{4}}{2^{8}\delta^{3}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{4}}^{4}+\delta,\quad&d=2,\\[4.30554pt]
\frac{3^{3}c^{4}}{2^{8}\delta^{3}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{6}}^{4}+\delta,\quad&d=3.\end{cases}

The positive constant
c
(given by the Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequalities) depends
only on the spatial domain
\mathscr{D}
.

Proof.

We first prove the result for
d=1
. By using Hölder’s inequality, we have

\left|\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{w},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle\right|\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}.

The following Gagliardo–Nirenberg interpolation inequality

\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{\infty}}\leq c\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{1/2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{1/2}

implies

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{w},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq c\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{1/2}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{1/2}+\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{1/2}\right)\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}

(A.7)

\displaystyle=c\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}+c\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{1/2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{3/2}.

The first term on the right-hand side of (
A.7
) can be estimated by

c\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\leq\frac{c^{2}}{\delta}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\delta}{4}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the second term on the right-hand side of (
A.7
) we use
Young’s inequality

ab\leq\frac{a^{p}}{p}+\frac{b^{q}}{q}\quad\text{where}\quad\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1,

with

a=\delta^{-3/4}c\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{1/2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}},\quad b=\delta^{3/4}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{3/2},\quad p=4,\quad\text{and}\quad q=4/3,

to obtain

c\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{1/2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{3/2}\leq\frac{c^{2}}{4\delta^{3}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{4}+\frac{3\delta}{4}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Thus, (
A.7
) gives

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{w},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq c^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4\delta^{3}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\delta\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Next we consider the case when
d=2
. Using Hölder’s inequality again
yields

\left|\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{w},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle\right|\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}.

The Gagliardo–Nirenberg interpolation inequality

\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{4}}\leq c\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{1/2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{1/2}

yields

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{w},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq c\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{1/2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{1/2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq c\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{1/2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{3/2}.

Using Young’s inequality again with

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{4\delta}{3}\right)^{-3/4}c\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{1/2},\qquad

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=4,

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{4\delta}{3}\right)^{3/4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{3/2},\quad

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=4/3,

we deduce

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{w},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3^{3}}{4^{4}\delta^{3}}c^{4}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{4}}^{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\delta\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{3^{3}}{4^{4}\delta^{3}}c^{4}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{4}}^{4}+\delta\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\delta\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Finally, we consider the case
d=3
. By Hölder’s inequality, we have

\left|\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{w},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle\right|\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{3}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{6}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}.

The Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality

\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{3}}\leq c\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{1/2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{1/2}

and Hölder’s inequality yield

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{w},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq c\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{6}}^{1/2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{1/2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{3/2}.

Using Young’s inequality, by the same argument as in the case
d=2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\nabla\boldsymbol{w},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle\right|\leq\left(\frac{3^{3}}{4^{4}\delta^{3}}c^{4}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{{L}^{6}}^{4}+\delta\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\delta\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}.

completing the proof of the lemma.
∎

Appendix B
Further regularity of the solution

In this section, we develop further a priori estimates on the approximate solution
\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}
to the
\epsilon
-LLBE defined in (
5.1
). Our aim is to derive estimates for
\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}
which are uniform in
\epsilon\in[0,1)
, but which possibly hold only locally in time, i.e. on
[0,T^{\ast}]
, where
T^{\ast}
is given by

(B.1)

\begin{cases}T^{\ast}=T,\quad&\text{if }d=1,2,\\
T^{\ast}\leq T,\quad&\text{if }d=3.\end{cases}

In particular, these estimates also imply further regularity properties for the solution of the
\epsilon
-LLBE and the LLBE.

Lemma B.1
.

Assume that (
5.2a
) and (
5.2b
) hold for
s=\max\{1,d-1\}
.
For any
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]
, where
T^{\ast}
is given by (
B.1
), there exists a constant
c
such that for every
n\in{N}
and
\epsilon\in[0,1)
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq c.

The constant
c
is independent of
n
and
\epsilon
, but may depend on
T^{\ast}
.

Proof.

Differentiating both sides of (
5.4a
) with respect
to
t
we obtain, for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial^{2}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle+\epsilon\left\langle\nabla\partial^{2}_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t),\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}+\kappa_{1}\left\langle\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t),\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}+\kappa_{2}\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\gamma\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}+\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)\times\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}

(B.2)

\displaystyle\quad-\kappa_{2}\mu\left\langle\partial_{t}(|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)|^{2})\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}-\kappa_{2}\mu\left\langle|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)|^{2}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}

where
\partial_{t}^{2}:=\partial^{2}/\partial t^{2}
.
Choosing
\boldsymbol{v}=2\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}
and integrating over
(0,t)
, we
deduce, after rearranging the terms,

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+2\kappa_{1}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+2\kappa_{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\kappa_{2}\mu\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\partial_{t}(|\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(s)|^{2})}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+2\kappa_{2}\mu\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(s)||\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(s)|}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

(B.3)

\displaystyle=\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}-2\gamma\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(s)\times\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(s),\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(s)\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s.

We use Lemma
A.3
with
\boldsymbol{v}=\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}
,

\boldsymbol{w}=\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}
, and
\delta=\kappa_{1}/4\gamma
to estimate the last term on
the right-hand side, and thus obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\partial_{t}(|\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(s)|^{2})}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(s)||\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(s)|}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

(B.4)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\Phi(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s))\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s,

where the constant is independent of
\epsilon
and
n
.

Now, for
d=1,2
, we repeat the arguments leading to (
3.12
). For
d=3
, we use the embedding
{H}^{2}\hookrightarrow{W}^{1,6}
and the
{H}^{2}
elliptic regularity to obtain

\displaystyle\Phi(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t))\lesssim 1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{H}^{2}}^{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{4}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{4}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{4}+\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the last step we used Lemma
5.1
.
Altogether, we have

(B.5)

\Phi(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t))\lesssim\begin{cases}1,\quad&d=1,\\
1+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2},\quad&d=2,\\
1+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2},\quad&d=3.\end{cases}

Noting (
B.5
), we continue from (
B.4
) and apply the Gronwall inequality. For
d=1,2
we use Lemma
5.1
, while for
d=3
we use Lemma
B.2
(which needs (
5.2a
) and (
5.2b
) to hold for
s=2
) to deduce that

(B.6)

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2},

for
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]
.
It remains to show the boundedness of
\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\epsilon}_{n}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}
in (
B.6
).
Letting
t\to 0^{+}
in (
5.4a
),
choosing
\boldsymbol{v}=\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)
, and rearranging the resulting equation, we
deduce after using Hölder’s inequality

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\kappa_{1}\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}+\gamma\boldsymbol{u}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}-\kappa_{2}(1+\mu|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}|^{2})\boldsymbol{u}_{0,n}^{\epsilon},\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}

(B.7)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0,n}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\right)\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}.

The embedding
{H}^{1}_{\Delta}\hookrightarrow{L}^{\infty}
and (
5.3
) yield

\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}},

which implies

\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\lesssim 1.

This, together with (
B.6
), proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Assume that (
5.2a
) and (
5.2b
) hold for
s=2
. For any
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]
, there exists a constant
c
such that for every
n\in{N}
and
\epsilon\in[0,1)
,

(B.8)

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq c.

The constant
c
in (
B.8
) is independent of
n
and
\epsilon
, but may depend
on
T^{\ast}
.

Proof.

To prove (
B.8
), we
set
\boldsymbol{v}=2\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}

in (
5.4a
) and obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+2\kappa_{1}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+2\kappa_{2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=-2\gamma\left\langle\nabla\Big(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)\Big),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}+2\kappa_{2}\mu\left\langle\nabla\Big(|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)\Big),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}.

The rest of the proof follows the argument in Lemma
3.4
almost verbatim, replacing
\boldsymbol{u}_{n}
by
\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}
, thus leading to the required estimate.
∎

Lemma B.3
.

Assume that (
5.2a
) and (
5.2b
) hold for
s=2
. For any
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]
and
\alpha
satisfying (
2.5
), there exists a constant
c
such that for every
n\in{N}
and
\epsilon\in[0,1)
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{H}^{1+\alpha}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq c,

where the constant
c
is independent of
n
and
\epsilon
, but may depend on
T^{\ast}
.

Proof.

Putting
\boldsymbol{v}=-2\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}
in (
B.2
) and integrating over
(0,t)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+2\kappa_{1}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+2\kappa_{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}-2\gamma\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s),\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+2\kappa_{2}\mu\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\partial_{t}\big(|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)|^{2}\big)\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)+|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)|^{2}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s),\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)\right\rangle_{{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s

(B.9)

\displaystyle=\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+T_{1}+T_{2}.

We will estimate the last two terms on the right-hand side. Firstly, by Hölder’s inequality we have

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2\gamma\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{4}}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{4}}\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq c\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{2}+\frac{\kappa_{1}}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s,

where in the last step we used Young’s inequality and Sobolev embedding
{H}^{1}\hookrightarrow{L}^{4}
. The constant
c
is independent of
\epsilon
. For the last term, by similar argument we have

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\kappa_{2}\mu\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{6}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{6}}^{2}\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq c\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{4}+\frac{\kappa_{1}}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s.

Substituting these into (
B.9
) and adding the term
\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}
on both sides give

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\kappa_{1}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+2\kappa_{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+c\int_{0}^{t}\left(1+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s,

where in the last step we used (
5.5
). By Gronwall’s inequality (noting Lemma
B.1
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq c\left(1+\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}1+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(s)}\right\|_{{H}^{1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\leq c\left(1+\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}\right),

where
c
is independent of
\epsilon
, and in the last step we used (
B.8
). To show the boundedness of
\left\|{\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)}\right\|_{{L}^{2}}^{2}
, we can follow the same argument as in (
B.7
) with
\boldsymbol{v}=\Delta\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}(0)
instead. This, together with an argument similar to (
3.15
), completes the proof.
∎

To summarise, we have the following estimates for the approximate solution
\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}
of the
\epsilon
-LLBE which are uniform in
\epsilon\in[0,1)
:

•

If (
5.2a
) holds for
s=\max\{1,d-1\}
, then

(B.10)

\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}({L}^{2})}+\sqrt{\epsilon}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}({H}^{1})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}({H}^{1})}\lesssim 1.

•

If, in addition, (
5.2a
) holds for
s=2
, then we also have

(B.11)

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}({L}^{2})}+\sqrt{\epsilon}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}({H}^{1})}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}({H}^{1})}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1,

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}({H}^{1})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}({H}^{1}_{\Delta})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}({H}^{1+\alpha})}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\epsilon}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}({H}^{1}_{\Delta})}+\sqrt{\epsilon}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}^{\epsilon}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T^{\ast}}({H}^{1+\alpha})}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1.

All constants on the right-hand sides of (
B.10
) and (
B.11
) are independent of
\epsilon
and
n
. Consequently, the same estimates (with
\epsilon=0
) also hold for the approximate solution
\boldsymbol{u}_{n}
of the LLBE.
